| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 850 | 2021-11-24 22:09:11.000000000 | Eventually yes, but we don't have any linear options yet in the spec. Input images can have arbitrary color profiles, so it's possible an input image could be labeled as linear-srgb, but it would be uncommon.  My bigger question is how we would handle arbitrary input color profiles. I don't know what the Chromium code looks like so I don't know what we would need to feed into Dawn. In practice I think images are just tagged with arbitrary embedded color profiles so I guess Chromium must be detecting those and figuring out how they map to predefined profiles like sRGB or Display-P3. As I discovered when I was trying to create test files there are multiple possible icc definitions for Display-P3 though: https://github.com/saucecontrol/Compact-ICC-Profiles (But instead of using one of these I used the profile from the WebKit Logo test image here https://webkit.org/blog-files/color-gamut/)  Maybe in the long term we need fully general support just like for sampling external textures. I imagine we could compute the color conversion constants on the CPU and pass them in a uniform buffer. Hopefully they could share the implementation.  Having the full matrix be computed ahead of time on the CPU would also avoid needing to do two conversions inside the shader at [1]. (But it's probably OK if we have two conversions for now.) |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 860 | 2021-12-01 23:23:17.000000000 | Agree, I guess originally we implemented this with a single precomputed alphaOp ("premultiply", "unpremultiply", or "none") but now that we have color conversions we need separate src and dst premultipliedAlpha states (each being true or false).  Unfortunately this is going to need changes in both Dawn and Blink but maybe we can smoothly transition with a few stages of changes. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 869 | 2021-12-01 23:23:17.000000000 | unrelated nit: Shouldn't the names be spelled "flip y" and "alpha op"? |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 872 | 2021-12-08 00:03:47.000000000 | nit: "sep*e*rate" -> "sep*a*rate".  That said though, I don't think this name is very clear. I prefer just "unpremultiplied". |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 880 | 2021-12-08 00:03:47.000000000 | Since we now have the generalized conversion code in the shader (nice!) does Dawn really need to understand sRGB and display p3? Could we just have Chromium pass the gamma parameters and conversion matrix instead? (Or maybe the conversion matrix and "is sRGB encoded" for src/dst)  This could simplify a few things:  - less or no work would be required in Dawn for new src color space support - we wouldn't need ValidateCopyTextureColorSpaceConversion - we would move all of the ColorSpaceTable data to Blink where it's probably already known? (Maybe this isn't true, I don't know if Chromium has it in Blink or in the GPU process. Maybe we would need to make this call from the GPU process which would be harder.)  Though it also makes the stepsMask more complicated to compute because we would need to compare the actual parameters passed in. Also we would still end up needing some ColorSpaceTable data in the tests.  (We should discuss this further before you make a change. Probably shouldn't do it in this CL but we could do it before integrating into Chromium if we want to avoid conflicts.) |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 880 | 2021-12-08 02:04:06.000000000 | I don't really know how those work either or how they're used. (The main thing I don't know is whether or how Chromium would get from a user-provided image file to one of these color spaces. Maybe it would always use the "CUSTOM" modes.)  The definitions for srgb and display-p3 are interesting, though (CreateSRGB and CreateDisplayP3D65). |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 880 | 2021-12-08 03:02:09.000000000 | Nice find!  Now thinking about it, it makes sense Chromium would be willing to do this in order to prevent the shader from being regenerated and recompiled. But since we don't regenerate the shader, I don't think it's super necessary for Dawn. I'm not so worried about copying over a few extra floats in the wire command.  It's interesting that the code you linked only detects when a profile == sRGB (but not Display-P3 or Adobe RGB or other common profiles). Maybe those are somewhere else.  I also don't know how common it is for user images to have ICC profiles that don't match one of the common profiles. But if Dawn supports only a few (like sRGB and Display-P3) then there will certainly be plenty of others that show up, at least Adobe RGB, Display P3, and ProPhoto like on https://webkit.org/blog-files/color-gamut/  OTOH the spec dictates a small possible list of _destination_ color spaces, so using presets there makes sense.  What I'm currently envisioning would have CopyTextureForBrowser pass the following:  - srcAlphaMode: enum { premul, unpremul } - srcTransferFunctionParameters: f32 A,B,C,E,F,G,epsilon - 3x3 f32 conversion matrix - dstTransferFunction: enum { linear, srgb } // doesn't make sense to specify the whole color space because it would make it non-orthogonal to the conversion matrix - dstAlphaMode: enum { premul, unpremul } |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | dawn.json | 880 | 2021-12-14 04:42:42.000000000 | Because destination is specified by the WebGPU spec and it can only be "srgb" right now (and later "srgb-linear" and "display-p3" probably). We could also just pass all of the parameters but it's not strictly necessary - in fact we could even hardcode the srgb numbers into the shader for the destination if we wanted to (I don't think we need to).  The parameters sound good to me. needColorSpaceConversion could possibly be determined by Dawn but maybe it's better to have it explicit. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 44 | 2021-12-02 22:29:19.000000000 | I'm not familiar enough with all of the possible transforms we'll need to do. I wrote this based on my understanding of your current shader but maybe it doesn't work well ultimately in the general case.  The color_transform code has the advantage that it's generating code so it doesn't need to worry about optimizing out unused parts. However I think we should avoid that if we can.  We can skip handling HDR for now, but I think we may need it relatively soon - though we don't have HDR canvas output yet, HDR video is well established and HDR video input is probably still useful as it can be used in HDR rendering pipelines.  Maybe a better way to generalize would look more like:   // 0x01 = unpremultiply (src is premultiplied)  // 0x02 = do gamma decoding (src is nonlinear)  // 0x04 = apply conversion matrix (color primaries are not the same)  // 0x08 = do gamma encoding (dst is nonlinear)  // 0x10 = premultiply (dst is premultiplied)  // (Inject more steps into this mask as needed)  u32 step_mask;  // TODO: Add info about which gamma encodings are used at each step, once it's not always sRGB   if (step_mask & 0x01) unpremultiply;  if (step_mask & 0x02) convert nonlinear to linear;  if (step_mask & 0x04) apply conversion matrix;  if (step_mask & 0x08) convert linear to nonlinear;  if (step_mask & 0x10) premultiply;  I'm not sure. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 44 | 2021-12-01 23:23:17.000000000 | With the matrix now precomputed, u_srcColorSpace and u_dstColorSpace should be in principle used only to derive some specific information:  - Is the conversion matrix an identity and therefore can be skipped? - What is the transfer function of src? - What is the transfer function of dst? - If the conversion matrix is an identity, can the transfer functions also be skipped?  Having the uniform inputs correspond to wgpu::ColorSpace confuses things a bit because that information is not really used directly.  I think I would have the final logic look something like:   // 3 = skip all, 2 = skip matrix and TF, 1 = skip matrix, 0 = skip none:  u32 conversion_mode;   // Later probably replaced with full a/b/c/d/e/f/g formulation:  u32 src_is_nonlinear, dst_is_nonlinear; // 0 or 1   u32 src_is_premul, dst_is_premul; // 0 or 1   if (conversion_mode < 3) { // Can't skip premul/unpremul    if (src_is_premul == 1) unpremultiply;    if (conversion_type < 2) { // Can't skip transfer function      if (src_is_nonlinear == 1) convert nonlinear to linear;      if (conversion_type < 1) { // Can't skip conversion matrix        apply conversion matrix;      }      if (dst_is_nonlinear == 1) convert linear to nonlinear;    }    if (dst_is_premul == 1) premultiply;  } |

So I think this implementation can be agnostic to which of the two it's used with and called something like "srgb_nonlinear_to_linear_positive". (since it doesn't handle negative numbers)  Also I don't know if there's a reason these constants differ slightly from the typical definition of sRGB, but, if possible, using the typical numbers would be clearer. (0.04045 instead of 4.04499359e-02, 1/12.92 instead of 7.73993805e-02, etc.) https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRGB |

It's similar to "codec" (compression/decompression).  "gamma-uncompressed" or "uncompressed" would be a good word here IMO as it will be more universally understood, but keeping the terminology from the CSS spec is also fine. |

| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 64 | 2021-11-29 23:52:49.000000000 | Hmm, this comment seems to say Chromium uses gamma ~2.2 for BT709, I don't think it says anything directly about sRGB. From reading that comment and Wikipedia, the gamma for Rec. 709 is not well-defined but is usually 2.4 or 2.2. So it makes sense for Chromium to pick ~2.2 and then use the sRGB transfer function (which is very close to 2.2) to match hardware.  I'm sure you noticed the actual code sets the value of 'g' to 2.4. But the overall gamma is close to 2.2 because of how sRGB is defined. This is explained by Wikipedia as follows:  > This curve is a slight tweaking of the simple Gamma 2.2 function.[5] A linear section is near zero, in order to avoid an infinite or zero slope that a gamma curve has. This is spliced to a curved section designed so the overall function is very close to a gamma 2.2 function. In order to do this a formula using a higher exponent (2.4 in this case) is necessary. The instantaneous gamma (the slope when plotted on a log:log scale) varies from 1 in the linear section to 2.4 at maximum intensity, with a median value being close to 2.2.  https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRGB  In any case, I realized why this constant is different: it's not in the same place. The nonlinear part of sRGB is:  1.055 * x^(1/2.4) - 0.055  which is equivalent to:  = ((1.055^2.4 * x)^(1/2.4) - 0.055 = (1.13711894 * x)^(1/2.4) - 0.055  So this does match the definition of sRGB on Wikipedia.  Looks like Chromium uses this alternate formulation so that more transfer functions (like sRGB and inverse sRGB I suspect) can be defined with constants in the same formula: https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/skia/include/third_party/skcms/skcms.h;l=39;drc=1e0c2a218eb71bef788fd87b0e7a4e9e93ecfe1d  This is really useful and we may want it for the future so our transfer function can be fully controlled by values in a uniform buffer for inputs like videos that may have many different color profiles. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 64 | 2021-11-24 22:09:11.000000000 | This looks like the sRGB linear-to-nonlinear conversion (see above), in which case it could be called something like "srgb_linear_to_nonlinear_positive".  However there's one difference with the sRGB conversion which is it uses 1.13711894 instead of 1.055. Is that difference intentional? |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 69 | 2021-12-01 23:23:17.000000000 | Architectures might prefer a "vectorized" version of this operation which operates on all three components in parallel, like:   ... (v: vec3<f32>) -> vec3<f32> {    let linear_part = vec3<f32>(12.92) * v;    let gamma_part = vec3<f32>(1.055) * pow(v, vec3<f32>(1.0 / 2.4)) - vec3<f32>(0.055);    return select(gamma_part, linear_part, v < vec<f32>(0.0031308));  }  Not very necessary to do now, but could try it, or leave a TODO to consider it. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 72 | 2021-12-08 00:03:47.000000000 | I don't quite understand why you need the colorSpaceIsExtended option at all. (Maybe just because I've been thinking only about srgb and display-p3.)  IIUC either colorSpaceIsExtended and you need to handle negative numbers, or it's not and you can pretend they don't exist. Here, I think the select() calls have no effect for this reason.  Also, they don't save any performance because sign() and abs() occur unconditionally. sign() and abs() are very cheap so I think you should just use them without the select() which will also be cheaper. |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 72 | 2021-12-08 02:04:06.000000000 | That change in the CSS spec is quite new, so that makes sense. And yes, we explicitly want to implement the latest CSS definitions.  Chromium is probably just omitting generation of sign()/abs() if it's not needed, for some performance. I don't suspect it's ever harmful to include them - it only makes a difference for negative values and I don't even know whether we can get negative display-p3 values into the conversion code in Chromium. (It seems very unlikely to me that anything is expecting negative values to have any other behavior anyway.) |
| [70780](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/70780) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 76 | 2021-12-01 23:23:17.000000000 | There isn't really consistency in English except in contexts where mathematical [inclusive,exclusive) style intervals are understood. Outside of math-y contexts 0.0-1.0 is much more common. I find in English if you just write [0, 1] or (0, 1) it's not always clear that it's an interval.  In this case I think 0.0-1.0, [0.0,1.0] and other variants are perfectly clear. |

| [74301](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/74301) | dawn | src/dawn_native/metal/CommandBufferMTL.mm | 907 | 2022-01-06 05:43:18.000000000 | IIUC the retainedReferences=NO (or commandBufferWithUnretainedReferences) change would be a larger change because we need to change how the entire command buffer is configured, it's not just local to here. Seems like it should be separate from this change.  However I think Corentin's comment is still correct. The problem I see is, CreateFormatView returns an NSPRef<id<MTLTexture>>. Then, you .Get() that. The problem is, I believe the NSPRef's destructor will run after .Get() but before [MTLBlitCommandEncoder copyFromTexture] is entered. So if the NSPRef is the last ref, then it can will [release] the id<MTLTexture> before Metal receives it and performs its own [retain].  Fix would just be:   NSPRef<id<MTLTexture>> dstTextureView =      dstTexture->CreateFormatView(srcTexture->GetFormat().format);  [... copyFromTexture ... toTexture:dstTextureView.Get() ...]  // dstTextureView goes out of scope AFTER [copyFromTexture] executes. |

| [74580](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/74580) | dawn | /COMMIT_MSG | 16 | 2022-01-04 23:55:35.000000000 | This is probably a good idea. If the destination format is -srgb and the destination color space is srgb-encoded then we can just skip the srgb conversion at the end. Unfortunately if the destination color space is not srgb-encoded then it requires extra code in the shader and that's why I didn't give this enough thought.  Now I'm thinking the spec should actually disallow copying into *-srgb with color spaces that aren't srgb-encoded (so "srgb" and "display-p3" are OK, but "srgb-linear" or "prophoto-rgb" are not). (Right now the only destination color space is "srgb", so we would put limitation in when we expand it.) |

| [74580](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/74580) | dawn | src/dawn_native/CopyTextureForBrowserHelper.cpp | 191 | 2022-01-06 00:55:34.000000000 | It's strange that this is after the premultiply step, but I realize that's technically correct. But premultiplication with an srgb destination format doesn't make any sense. I think it should also be disallowed in the spec. I commented this on https://github.com/gpuweb/gpuweb/issues/1715#issuecomment-1006192707.  If premultiplication is disallowed, then we shouldn't need an extra step in the shader here: kDecodeForSrgbDstFormat can cancel out with kEncodeToGammaStep. Then instead of introducing the extra step you just modify the steps_mask.  IMO, we shouldn't block this CL. We can do that in a follow-up patch (perhaps after the spec has this restriction). It will be an incremental change on top of this one: a self-contained optimization that will stand well on its own as a separate PR. |

| [208734](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/208734) | dawn | src/dawn/native/vulkan/PipelineLayoutVk.cpp | 114 | 2024-10-08 14:17:21.000000000 | We create a pipeline layout with an `internalImmediateDataSize` of 0 here every time a new PipelineLayout is created which seems useful to pre-initialize the cache.  Every `(Render\\|Compute)Pipeline::InitializeImpl` also initializes the layout with an `internalImmediateDataSize` of `kClampFragDepthArgsSize`. I think this would result in a minimum of 2 `VkPipelineLayout`s being created every time.  You could add a `constexpr size_t kDefaultInternalImmediateDataSize = kClampFragDepthArgsSize + ...`. You can also assert that when the user push constant size is 0, the default matches the size that `*Pipeline::InitializeImpl` uses:  ```     // TODO(crbug.com/366291600): Add internal immediate data size when needed.     constexpr uint32_t userImmediateDataSize = 0;     constexpr uint32_t internalImmediateDataSize = kClampFragDepthArgsSize;     DAWN_ASSERT(internalImmediateDataSize == kDefaultInternalImmediateDataSize);     DAWN_TRY(InitializeBase(layout, internalImmediateDataSize + userImmediateDataSize)); ``` |

| [219634](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/219634) | dawn | src/dawn/native/ImmediateConstantsLayout.h | 78 | 2025-01-08 16:51:28.000000000 | The changes in RenderPipelineVk.cpp look like they only need to know about the index and count of the immediate constants used which can still be calculated with a `offsetof` and `sizeof` from the `RenderImmediateConstants` structs. ex:  `RenderPipelineBase::RenderPipelineBase` would look like:  ``` RenderPipelineBase::RenderPipelineBase(     DeviceBase* device,     const UnpackedPtr<RenderPipelineDescriptor>& descriptor,     ImmediateConstantMask requiredInternalImmediateConstants) ```  which would eliminate some of the enumeration of the constants and make them more generic.  At some point, the pipeline needs to know if it needs the immediate constant or not. Something like this in ` RenderPipelineBase::RenderPipelineBase`: ``` // Gather list of immediate constants used by this pipeline ImmediateConstantMask pipelineConstants; if (GetLayout()->GetImmediateDataRangeByteSize()) {     // Also add these bits to the required bits?     pipelineConstants \\|= GetImmediateConstantBits(offsetof(RenderPipeline, userConstants), sizeof(UserImmediateConstants)); } if (UsesFragDepth() && !HasUnclippedDepth()) {     pipelineConstants \\|= GetImmediateConstantBits(offsetof(RenderPipeline, clampFragDepth), sizeof(ClampFragDepthConstants)); } if (UsesVertexIndex()) {     pipelineConstants \\|= GetImmediateConstantBits(offsetof(RenderPipeline, firstVertex), sizeof(uint32_t));     pipelineConstants \\|= GetImmediateConstantBits(offsetof(RenderPipeline, firstInstance), sizeof(uint32_t)); }  uint32_t offset = 0; for (auto index : IterateBitSet(pipelineConstants & requiredInternalImmediateConstants)) {     mImmediateConstantOffsetMap.insert({static_cast<uint32_t>(index), offset});     offset += kImmediateConstantElementByteSize; }  mImmediateConstantMask \\|= immediateConstantBlockBits; ```  where `GetImmediateConstantBits` just looks like ``` // Convert byte sizes and offests into immediate constant indicies and offsets // (dividing everything by kImmediateConstantElementByteSize) ImmediateConstantMask GetImmediateConstantBits(size_t byteOffset, size_t byteSize) {     size_t firstIndex = byteOffset / kImmediateConstantElementByteSize;     size_t constantCount = byteSize / kImmediateConstantElementByteSize;      ImmediateConstantMask bits;     for (size_t bit = firstIndex; bit < firstIndex + constantCount; bit++) {         // This could be done with bit logic only...         bits.set(bit);     }     return bits; } ```  Please push back if this looks like it wont work. I think avoiding having all this metadata about the constants is a nice win though. |

| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 115 | 2025-04-24 15:44:44.000000000 | It's fine to compare mBits too but not necessary I think. Iterators are only supposed to be compared when they iterate over the same data. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 115 | 2025-04-23 21:32:36.000000000 | Both comparison operators should compare mOffset and mSize. Generally it's only valid to compare two iterators over the same collection (the bitset) so we don't need to compare that.  This should return true when two iterators represent the same range of bits. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 125 | 2025-04-23 21:32:36.000000000 | This should be constexpr if possible. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 131 | 2025-03-21 16:30:19.000000000 | I would call this simply `advance`. All the call sites look the same: ```     auto range = getNextRange();     mOffset = range.first;     mSize = range.second; ``` so you can also move the mOffset and mSize setting into this function. I would also shift mBits so that the 0th bit is at offset+size. We would store:   * current offset  * current size  * bits that have been shifted to offset + size and are ready to have `advance` called again.   We could probably avoid needing `mWordOffset` because the bits we want to look at always start at the first bit.   This means that the iterator will be comparable to the `end` iterator without any processing once there are no ranges left. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 137 | 2025-03-21 16:30:19.000000000 | ANGLE has overloads for ScanForward [0] which work on 64 bits with fallbacks for 32-bit systems. Using those would likely be much faster since we do have a bunch of bitsets in the 32-64 bit size. You would use `to_ullong` instead to get the values out of the std::bitset.  [0] https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/dawn/third_party/angle/src/common/mathutil.h;l=1220-1240;drc=f5196a27b9b6bdc358191717f2b5a3ba824d1e82 and https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/dawn/third_party/angle/src/common/mathutil.h;l=1283-1293;drc=f5196a27b9b6bdc358191717f2b5a3ba824d1e82 |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 147 | 2025-03-21 16:30:19.000000000 | I think we can avoid the loop here. If we invert `remainingBits` then we can use ScanForward on it again to find the next `0` bit.  The algorithm then becomes roughly: ``` offset = ScanForward(bits) bits = bits >> offset bits = ~bits size = ScanForward(bits) // Here we need to handle wrapping to the next word, we will know because there will be a 1 bit at the end of our data due to the ~ bits = bits >> size bits = ~bits ```  I would refactor some of these into helper functions that are marked inline. Something like `uint32_t findNext1Bit()` which handles the word wrapping would help a lot here. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 161 | 2025-04-23 21:32:36.000000000 | I think we can refactor some parts of this function. The part that scans a bitset and shifts it can be done together in a function like (untested):  ``` template <size_t N> size_t ScanForwardAndShiftBits(std::bitset<N>& bits) {     if (bits.none()) {         return N; // Or some other indicator that there are no bits.     }      constexpr std::bitset<N> wordMask(std::numeric_limits<uint64_t>::max());     size_t offset = 0;     while ((bits & wordMask).to_ullong() == 0) {         offset += kBitsPerWord;         bits >>= kBitsPerWord;     }      size_t nextBit = ScanForward((bits & wordMask).to_ullong());     bits >>= nextBit;     return offset + nextBit; } ```  Then I think we can write this function using that helper:  ``` // Bits are currently shifted to mOffset + mSize. mOffset += mSize;  size_t rangeStart = ScanForwardAndShiftBits(mBits); if (rangeStart == N) {     // Reached the end, no more ranges.     mOffset == N;     mSize == 0;     return; }  mOffset += rangeStart; mBits = ~mBits;  size_t rangeCount = ScanForwardAndShiftBits(mBits); if (rangeCount == N) {     // All bits until the end of the set are set.     rangeEnd = N - mOffset; }  mSize = rangeCount; mBits = ~mBits; ``` |

| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/common/BitSetRangeIterator.h | 163 | 2025-04-24 15:44:44.000000000 | What's the purpose of these shifts? |

Lets make each test use different sizes.  Please cover small sets with < 1 word and very large ones > 128 bits. |
| [231754](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231754) | dawn | src/dawn/tests/unittests/BitSetRangeIteratorTests.cpp | 97 | 2025-03-21 16:30:19.000000000 | nit: Could be: ``` auto iterator = IterateBitSetRanges(mStateBits); EXPECT_EQ(iterator.end(), iterator.begin()); ``` |

| [231774](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231774) | dawn | src/dawn/native/Adapter.cpp | 101 | 2025-03-25 13:59:03.000000000 | This is still the same function. It should be `ApplyLimitTiers(&mLimits)` |
| [231774](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/231774) | dawn | src/dawn/native/Limits.h | 60 | 2025-03-21 16:40:42.000000000 | Either way we're copying the input explicitly from a const& or implicitly from a passing by value.  In this case we're assigning it back to the source so let's add an overload:  `void ApplyLimitTiers(CombinedLimits* limits)` which does the in-place updates.  `CombinedLimits ApplyLimitTiers(CombinedLimits limits)` can be rewritten as: ``` CombinedLimits ApplyLimitTiers(const CombinedLimits& limits) {     CombinedLimits limitsCopy = limits;     ApplyLimitTiers(&limitsCopy);     return limitsCopy; } ``` |

| [219634](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/219634) | dawn | src/dawn/native/ImmediateConstantsTracker.h | 57 | 2025-01-07 22:08:52.000000000 | Maybe we can have a `Set` function as well on this which sets the data and returns `true` iff we overrode something? Then in the the `Set*` functions we could just call with the offset from `*ByteLayouts`?  i.e.  ``` template <typename In> bool Set(uint32_t offset, In in) {     DAWN_ASSERT(sizeof(In) + offset <= sizeof(T));     if (memcmp(Get<In>(offset), &in), sizeof(In)) != 0) {         *reinterpret_cast<In*>(&mData[offset]) = in;         return true;     }     return false; } ```  Then in something like `SetNumWorkgroups`, we could have:  ``` SetNumWorkgroups(uint32_t numWorkgroupX,                  uint32_t numWorkgroupY,                  uint32_t numWorkgroupZ) {     bool constantBlockDirty = false;      constantBlockDirty \\|=         mContent.Set(             ComputeImmediateConstantsBytesLayout::NumWorkgroupsX,             numWorkgroupX         );     constantBlockDirty \\|=         mContent.Set(             ComputeImmediateConstantsBytesLayout::NumWorkgroupsY,             numWorkgroupY         );     constantBlockDirty \\|=         mContent.Set(             ComputeImmediateConstantsBytesLayout::NumWorkgroupsZ,             numWorkgroupZ         );     ... } ``` |

I'm thinking that (at least since this is in C++), the `rangeOffset` could just be passed in via `values + rangeOffset` to begin with?   Main reason I'm asking is because having both offsets makes it a bit confusing to use and users could easily mistake one offset for the other leading to unintended behavior? |

| [235154](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/235154) | dawn | src/dawn/native/Limits.h | 39 | 2025-04-09 20:26:31.000000000 | Yea, I think it would only really make sense to do it for descriptors that have trivial defaults where we are already making a copy of the descriptors. |
| [235154](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/235154) | dawn | src/dawn/native/Limits.h | 39 | 2025-04-08 18:24:02.000000000 | We already have something like this via `Unpack` and `UnpackedPtr`. I think the one issue atm with `UnpackedPtr` is that it doesn't make a copy of the underlying structs, and as a result probably can't be used as a drop in replacement for `CombinedLimits` yet.  I've been thinking about updating `UnpackedPtr` to actually make a copy of, and own all the structs it unpacks, but was worried that it would be too much copying, however, when I was doing the defaulting of stuff, I realized that we also have this `WithTrivialFrontendDefaults` helper that already has to make a copy of the structs, and was considering merging the two.  I think that if I merge them, then `CombinedLimits` could just become an `UnpackedPtr<Limits>` (or equivalent in case I rename it when it copies), and we can just set the default in `dawn.json` which should in turn set the defaults. |

## rharrison@chromium.org

| Change | Project | File | Line | Updated | Message |
|---|---|---|---:|---|---|
| [221574](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/221574) | dawn | /PATCHSET_LEVEL |  | 2025-01-09 15:54:35.000000000 | So non-obviously this is actually a WGSL fuzzer issue, since it runs the IR fuzzers via a wrapper (i.e. WGSL->IR->IR fuzzer)  The general discussion of the fuzzers can be found here (https://source.chromium.org/chromium/chromium/src/+/main:third_party/dawn/docs/tint/fuzzers.md), though those instructions detail using the fuzzing tool wrapper script which I don't normally use.  (I am ignoring the wrapper script for the instructions below)  You probably can get away building using GN in a Dawn checkout (There are CMake flags for the fuzzers that you can use also, but that isn't actually what ClusterFuzz uses).  You will need a args.gn that includes the following lines: ``` ... use_libfuzzer = true  tint_build_ir_binary = true tint_build_ir_fuzzer = true ... ```  and then need to build `tint_wgsl_fuzzer`  You can then run the fuzzer using that command and passing in the test case via a filepath on the command line. You should be able to download the test case file from the original CF issue.  Without your change it should hit an issue when running and generate a big backtrace, and with the fix the output should be short just saying it ran one test case and didn't find anything.  (I have checked, and confirmed that the original issue reproduces in a Dawn standalone build, but for other issues, sometimes they don't, so you need to build in a full Chromium checkout using the args.gn at the bottom of the CF issue) |
| [221574](https://dawn-review.googlesource.com/c/dawn/+/221574) | dawn | /PATCHSET_LEVEL |  | 2025-01-13 15:24:23.000000000 | You are correct, that confirms your fix works 👍 |
